<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
The worker addressed both warnings I raised:

1. **Batch encoding**: The `reeval_checkpoint` function now has batch encoding:
   - `batch_size=64` parameter
   - `_batch_encode` helper that processes texts in batches with tqdm progress
   - Separate batch encoding for queries, positives, and negatives
   - Efficient negative sim computation: all negatives encoded in one pass, then matched to their queries via index mapping
   - Proper memory cleanup: `del neg_embs, neg_query_embs, all_neg_sims`

   This should reduce the 18-37h estimate to roughly 2-4h (64x fewer forward pass calls, minus padding overhead).
   
2. **Adaptive sim bins**: The code computes dynamic bin edges from the positive similarity distribution instead of using fixed thresholds, which solves the clustering problem where model similarities bunch up around 0.985. It then reassigns all results into these adaptive low/medium/high categories.

I'm checking how the bin edges are calculated—they're likely percentile-based to ensure balanced samples across each bin.
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Both fixes are well-implemented:

1. **Batch encoding**: 
   - `batch_size=64` parameter with `_batch_encode` helper
   - Separate batch passes for queries, positives, negatives
   - Efficient index-based mapping for negative similarities
   - Memory cleanup with `del`

2. **Adaptive sim bins**:
   - Triggers only when: not dry_run AND user didn't specify custom `--sim-bins`
   - Uses `np.percentile([0, 33.3, 66.7, 100])` to create terciles — each bin gets ~1/3 of samples
   - Updates the bins in-place, reassigns them across all results, and logs the edges to the config output for visibility.
```

{

```text
Worker findings:

1. **M1 reextract v2**: Still running. Logs show active CodeQL queries across multiple repos (sqlfluff, bleach, conan-io, MONAI). All.jsonl now has 44,173 lines — this matches the original expected total (44,173 from m1_extraction). But the original extraction had 15,897 structural data entries. So the reextract is appending to all.jsonl, and the current 44,173 lines could be:
   - Original 15,897 successful + ~28,276 being reprocessed = 44,173 (the original total before filtering)
   
   Looking at the original metrics more carefully: of the 44,173 instances attempted, only 15,897 succeeded (CodeQL + beniget fallback combined), while 28,276 failed extraction. The reextract is now processing those 28,276 failures and appending results to all.jsonl, which explains why the file has grown to 44,173 lines total.

But I'm realizing there's a potential issue with duplicates — if the original all.jsonl contained all attempted instances (both successes and failures), then appending the reextract results would create duplicates for the failed cases. The question is whether the original extraction only wrote successful extractions to all.jsonl, or if it included failures too.

Looking at the registry notes, the reextract is appending to all.jsonl in append mode. The math checks out: ~15,897 original lines plus ~28,276 from reextract equals the current 44,173 total. This suggests the reextract is successfully recovering those failed instances without creating duplicates, which means the original file likely only contained the successful extractions.

I'm also seeing updates to the worker registry — the m1_reextract_v2 has Director notes about not rerunning M2 and using it as a pilot, while the m2_full_qwen_continued is progressing at step 650 with eval metrics showing R@1=0.6009, and the starcoder variant is at step 500 with slower progress.

Disk space dropped from 112GB to 98GB free, losing about 14GB likely from reextract outputs and checkpoint growth, but that's still a healthy margin. I'm also clarifying something about the registry values — the 0.5501 R@1 for random_s43 is the final eval value, not the best checkpoint value, which was actually 0.5554 at step 1500. The checkpoint save message at final eval is likely just the standard final checkpoint logic triggering, not necessarily indicating a new best—since the final R@1 of 0.5501 is lower than the actual best of 0.5554 from step 1500, the best_by_recall_at_1 checkpoint should still point to that earlier step. So the worker's reported best_R1 of 0.5501 for s43 appears to be the final eval value rather than the true best.

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Good, now I have the full picture. The worker did a comprehensive progress check and updated registry entries.

Key findings from my independent verification:

**StarCoder s43 eval@500 is now available!**
- R@1 = 0.4708, val_loss = 0.5279, R@5 = 0.9469
- vs pilot s42: R@1 = 0.454 (at step 1000, best)
- At the same checkpoint (step 500), pilot s42 was R@1 = 0.435
- So s43 at step 500 (R@1=0.4708) > s42 at step 500 (R@1=0.435) — +3.6pp improvement


- This mirrors the consistent gains we saw with Qwen on full data.

**Qwen s44** is progressing steadily at step 850/2816, with eval@1000 coming up soon. **M1 reextract v2** is nearly done at 80.8% completion with an ETA around 15:00 CST May 15 — the registry shows 40,225 output JSON files, which represents individual files per instance rather than per repository. I also need to correct the registry notes for Qwen s43's best R@1 value.

The worker fixed the registry value I flagged — Qwen s43 now correctly shows 0.5554. Comparing StarCoder s43 versus s42 at eval@500 reveals a much sharper improvement than what Qwen showed: StarCoder gained 3.6 percentage points (0.435 to 0.4708) while Qwen only improved 0.3 percentage points (0.533 to 0.5357). This suggests StarCoder is more sensitive to seed variation at this early checkpoint, though the different validation splits across seeds make direct comparison tricky. StarCoder s43 at step 600 shows a loss of 0.5706, which is a spike but normal for individual batch losses.

The registry updates look solid overall — the worker included comprehensive metrics like GPU temperature, uptime, and step progress. I'm noticing an interesting discrepancy in the M1 reextract: the worker estimates 60 total hours with 42% progress by elapsed time (25 hours out of 60), but the instance count shows 80.8% completion. This gap likely means the remaining instances, particularly large repos like sqlfluff with over 1200 instances, take significantly longer to process due to their larger CodeQL databases.

I should write up a brief review highlighting the StarCoder s43 eval@500 result and the M1 reextract progress discrepancy.
```

Jimenez, John Yang, Alexander Wettig, Shunyu Yao, Kexin Pei, Ofir Press, Karthik Narasimhan\\\"\\n        venue: ICLR\\n        year: 2024\\n        relation: baseline\\n        summary: \\\"从 GitHub issues 构建 repo-level bug fixing benchmark，成为自动修复领域标准评估集。\\\"\\n\\n      - title: \\\"SWE-agent: Agent-Computer Interfaces Enable Automated Software Engineering\\\"\\n        authors: \\\"John Yang, Carlos E. Jimenez, Alexander Wettig, Kilian Lieret, Shunyu Yao, Karthik Narasimhan, Ofir Press\\\"\\n        venue: NeurIPS\\n        year: 2024\\n        relation: competitor\\n        summary: \\\"基于 LLM agent 的交互式 bug fixing 系统，定义了 ACI 接口范式。\\\"\\n\\n      - title: \\\"AutoCodeRover: Autonomous Program Improvement\\\"\\n        authors: \\\"Yuntong Zhang, Haifeng Ruan, Zhiyu Fan, Abhik Roychoudhury\\\"\\n        venue: ISSTA\\n        year: 2024\\n        relation: competitor\\n        summary: \\\"结合代码搜索与 spectrum-based fault localization，利用程序分析缩小修复范围。\\\"\\n\\n      - title: \\\"Agentless: Demystifying LLM-based Software Engineering Agents\\\"\\n        authors: \\\"Chunqiu Steven Xia, Yinlin Deng, Soren Dunn, Lingming Zhang\\\"\\n        venue: arXiv\\n        year: 2024\\n        relation: competitor\\n        summary: \\\"无 agent 架构的 two-phase localize-then-repair 方法，在 SWE-bench 上取得强 baseline。\\\"\\n\\n      - title: \\\"SWE-smith: Scaling Data for Software Engineering Agents\\\"\\n        authors: \\\"John Yang, Kilian Lieret, Carlos E. Jimenez, Alexander Wettig, Kabir Khandpur, Yanzhe Zhang, Binyuan Hui, Ofir Press, Ludwig Schmidt, Diyi Yang\\\"\\n        venue: \\\"NeurIPS (Datasets & Benchmarks, Spotlight)\\\"\\n        year: 2025\\n        relation: building-block\\n        summary: \\\"自动从 GitHub 生成大规模 SWE-bench 风格训练数据，StructFuse 用其 repos 做 data-flow 提取。\\\"\\n\\n      - title: \\\"RepoBench: Benchmarking Repository-Level Code Auto-Completion Systems\\\"\\n        authors: \\\"Tianyang Liu, Canwen Xu, Julian McAuley\\\"\\n        venue: ICLR\\n        year: 2024\\n        relation: baseline\\n        summary: \\\"Repo-level 代码补全/检索 benchmark，含 RepoBench-R 检索子任务。\\\"\\n\\n      - title: \\\"SweRank: Software Issue Localization with Code Ranking\\\"\\n        authors: \\\"Revanth Gangi Reddy, Tarun Suresh, JaeHyeok Doo, Ye Liu, Xuan Phi Nguyen, Yingbo Zhou, Semih Yavuz, Caiming Xiong, Heng Ji, Shafiq Joty\\\"\\n        venue: arXiv\\n        year: 2025\\n        relation: competitor\\n        summary: \\\"Retrieve-and-rerank 框架用于 software issue localization，在 SWE-Bench-Lite 和 LocBench 上 SOTA，text-based retrieval baseline。\\\"\\n\\n      - title: \\\"SWE-Fixer: Training Open-Source LLMs for Effective and Efficient GitHub Issue Resolution\\\"\\n        authors: \\\"Chengxing Xie, Bowen Li, Chang Gao, He Du, Wai Lam, Difan Zou, Kai Chen\\\"\\n        venue: \\\"ACL (Findings)\\\"\\n        year: 2025\\n        relation: competitor\\n        summary: \\\"BM25 + LLM retriever 的 coarse-to-fine file retrieval pipeline，在 SWE-Bench Verified 上 30.2%。\\\"\\n\\n  # ==========================================================================\\n  # Category 2: Hard Negative Mining in Contrastive Learning\\n  # ==========================================================================\\n  hard_negative_mining:\\n    description: \\\"对比学习中 hard negative 挖掘策略，以及密集检索中的负样本选择\\\"\\n    papers:\\n\\n      - title: \\\"Dense Passage Retrieval for Open-Domain Question Answering\\\"\\n        authors: \\\"Vladimir Karpukhin, Barlas Oguz, Sewon Min, Patrick Lewis, Ledell Wu, Sergey Edunov, Danqi Chen, Wen-tau Yih\\\"\\n        venue: EMNLP\\n        year: 2020\\n        relation: background\\n        summary: \\\"DPR 开创了 in-batch negatives + BM25 hard negatives 的双塔检索范式。\\\"\\n\\n      - title: \\\"Approximate Nearest Neighbor Negative Contrastive Learning for Dense Text Retrieval\\\"\\n        authors: \\\"Lee Xiong, Chenyan Xiong, Ye Li, Kwok-Fung Tang, Jialin Liu, Paul Bennett, Junaid Ahmed, Arnold Overwijk\\\"\\n        venue: ICLR\\n        year: 2021\\n        relation: background\\n        summary: \\\"ANCE 用 ANN index 动态挖掘 embedding-distance hard negatives，StructFuse 的 baseline negative 策略之一。\\\"\\n\\n      - title: \\\"A Simple Framework for Contrastive Learning of Visual Representations\\\"\\n        authors: \\\"Ting Chen, Simon Kornblith, Mohammad Norouzi, Geoffrey Hinton\\\"\\n        venue: ICML\\n        year: 2020\\n        relation: background\\n        summary: \\\"SimCLR 视觉对比学习奠基工作，证明 hard negatives 和大 batch size 对对比学习的重要性。\\\"\\n\\n      - title: \\\"Sentence-BERT: Sentence Embeddings using Siamese BERT-Networks\\\"\\n        authors: \\\"Nils Reimers, Iryna Gurevych\\\"\\n        venue: EMNLP\\n        year: 2019\\n        relation: background\\n        summary: \\\"Siamese/triplet 网络训练句子嵌入，StructFuse 的对比训练框架与其类似。\\\"\\n\\n      - title: \\\"Curriculum Learning for Dense Retrieval Distillation\\\"\\n        authors: \\\"Hansi Zeng, Hamed Zamani, Vishwa Vinay\\\"\\n        venue: \\\"SIGIR (Best Short Paper)\\\"\\n        year: 2022\\n        relation: background\\n        summary: \\\"CL-DRD: 课程学习式知识蒸馏用于密集检索，从粗粒度到细粒度逐步增加训练难度。\\\"\\n\\n      - title: \\\"Effective Hard Negative Mining for Contrastive Learning-Based Code Search\\\"\\n        authors: \\\"Fan Y., Li C., Ge J., Huang L., Luo B.\\\"\\n        venue: \\\"ACM TOSEM\\\"\\n        year: 2025\\n        relation: contrast-target\\n        summary: \\\"CoCoHaNeRe: 提出可微分 hard negative 机制用于代码搜索对比学习。其对 hard negative 机制的处理与 StructFuse 的发现形成关键对照——StructFuse 证明 true embedding-distance mining 即可持续提升性能。\\\"\\n        note: \\\"关键引用：StructFuse Claim 1 的 correction target。CoCoHaNeRe 论点是非可微 embedding negatives 学习'slow and ineffective'，StructFuse 证明用 true embedding distance mining 仍然有效。\\\"\\n\\n  # ==========================================================================\\n  # Category 3: Contrastive Learning for Code\\n  # ========================\\n\\n... _source line 842 · 2026-05-14T21:29:10.836Z_

This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   This is Loop #9 of a Research Reviewer session for the `structfuse-context` project. The reviewer is an independent scientific auditor that:
   - Monitors running experiments on server bjb1-42106 (2× RTX PRO 6000 96GB, cuda:0 and cuda:1)
   - Verifies experiment conclusions against actual data using only read-only tools
   - Reports findings to Director via `reviewer_post_to_director` (only output channel)
   - Reviews worker task completions for correctness
   - Enforces GPU whitelist (cuda:0, cuda:1 only), checkpoint integrity, and disk safety
   
   The session received 8 sequential `worker_done` notifications and reviewed each one, plus one progress monitoring pass. All responses are in Chinese per session instructions.

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **LoRA contrastive training**: PEFT LoRA (r=16, q_proj/v_proj) on Qwen2.5-Coder-3B and StarCoder2-3B
   - **Negative mining strategies**: random, embedding_distance, dataflow_aware, bm25 — compared via R@1/R@5/MRR
   - **Stratified analysis**: 5+2 dimensions (repo_size, pool_size, similarity, dataflow, neg_difficulty + pool_size_controlled + dataflow×pool_size cross)
   - **Dry-run mock predictions**: Simulated R@1 values used for pipeline validation, explicitly NOT real model outputs
   - **Confound analysis**: Rich-dataflow instances concentrated in large repos (99.3%); pool_size identified as true confound (R@1 ≈ 1-exp(-5/pool))
   - **Adapter-only checkpoints**: 5.8GB→~200MB, loaded with strict=False
   - **L2-normalized embeddings**: contrastive_model.py line 130 `F.normalize(pooled, p=2, dim=-1)`, making dot product = cosine similarity
   - **Dual checkpoint strategy**: best_by_val_loss and best_by_recall_at_1 saved independently
   - **BM25 watcher processes**: Automatic chaining — watchers monitor training chain PIDs, start BM25 training when M2 chains complete
   - **M1 reextract v2**: Recovering 28,276 failed CodeQL extraction instances, 4 CPU workers parallel
   - **PEP 456 hash randomization**: Caused v1→v2 stratified analysis numerical differences (mock predictions are hash-dependent)

3. Files and Code Sections:
   - `/root/structfuse/evaluation/analyze_stratified.py` (1308 lines, most recently modified)
     - Central file for stratified evaluation of M2 strategies
     - Three modes: `--dry-run` (mock), `--predictions-dir`, `--checkpoints-dir` (new)
     - `reeval_checkpoint` (line 161+): Batch encoding with batch_size=64, _batch_encode helper, proper L2-norm dot product ranking
     - `run_pool_size_controlled_analysis` (line 484+): Within-bin delta + count-weighted aggregate to control pool_size confound
     - `run_dataflow_pool_cross_analysis` (line 558+): Cross-tabulation with min_cell_size=30 underpowered flagging
     - Adaptive sim bins (line 1201+): `np.percentile([0, 33.3, 66.7, 100])` terciles for non-dry-run mode, overrides SIMILARITY_BINS
     - Config output records `similarity_bin_edges`, `batch_size`, `dry_run` flags

   - `/root/autodl-tmp/structfuse/artifacts/stratified_dryrun_v2/` (stratified_results.json, stratified_summary.csv, stratified_plots.pdf)
     - v2 dry-run output with 5 dimensions, 2980 val samples (seed 42)
     - All R@1 values are mock predictions — CANNOT be cited in paper
     - Consistent ranking: dataflow > embedding > random across all strata

   - `/root/autodl-tmp/structfuse/artifacts/confound_analysis/` (confound_report.md, controlled_comparison.csv, cross_tabulation.csv)
     - Cross-tabulation confirms 99.3% of rich-dataflow in large/xlarge repos
     - Controlled comparison: rich < moderate R@1 persists in 3/3 bins (medium/large/xlarge)
     - Effect sizes small in large/xlarge (~2pp), large in medium but only 99 samples
     - Explicitly states: "R@1 values are from dry-run mock predictions (not real model)"

   - `/root/autodl-tmp/structfuse/artifacts/bm25_overlap_stratified.json`
     - BM25 overlap by repo size: small 88%, medium 68%, large 28%
     - BM25 provides genuinely different negatives only in large repos

   - `/root/structfuse/training/contrastive_model.py` (194 lines)
     - Line 130: `return F.normalize(pooled, p=2, dim=-1)` — L2 normalization confirmed
     - Line 117: `def encode(self, input_ids, attention_mask)` — shared encoder

   - `/root/runs/m2_full_qwen/random_s44.log`
     - eval@500: val_loss=0.4252, R@1=0.6009, R@5=0.9583 (anomalously high)
     - Last seen at step 850, loss=0.2837

   - `/root/runs/m2_full_starcoder/random_s43.log`
     - eval@500: val_loss=0.5279, R@1=0.4708, R@5=0.9469
     - Last seen at step 600, loss=0.5706

   - `/root/runs/m2_full_starcoder/s43plus_all.log`
     - Shows first StarCoder s43 attempt killed (PID 442120), resume script correctly replaced it

   - Literature map: `/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_5/projects/structfuse-context/literature_map.yaml`
     - 37 entries across categories: code retrieval for bug fixing, hard negative mining, contrastive learning for code, etc.

4. Errors and Fixes:
   - **ssh_read_only pipe character blocking**: Multiple grep commands with `|` were blocked by security policy. Fixed by using separate grep calls without pipes.
   - **Registry filter by exp_id**: `filters={"exp_id": "plan_005_stratified"}` returned 0 results. The filter syntax may require exact field matching; used limit-based queries instead.
   - **Qwen s43 best_R1 misrecording**: Worker recorded 0.5501 (final eval) instead of 0.5554 (best checkpoint at step 1500). Reported as warning; subsequently corrected by the monitoring worker.
   - **Non-existent changelog reference**: Registry hypothesis referenced `artifacts/v1_v2_changelog.md` that was never created. Reported as warning.

5. Problem Solving:
   - **Stratified analysis credibility**: Discovered ALL R@1 values in stratified analysis are mock predictions (dry-run). This was a CRITICAL finding that changed the assessment from "strong evidence for Claim 2" to "tentative pending real model validation." Successfully escalated and adopted by worker team.
   - **Dataflow richness paradox**: Identified that rich-dataflow instances have lower R@1 than moderate. Through confound analysis, traced to pool_size (not repo_size) as the true confound. Rich instances have larger candidate pools (46-50 files vs 17-37), mechanically reducing R@1.
   - **analyze_stratified.py improvements**: Identified two issues (no batch encoding, wrong sim bins for real eval) that were subsequently fixed by worker. Verified fixes were correct.
   - **StarCoder process confusion**: Multiple train.py PIDs on cuda:1 initially appeared as duplicate training. Traced via ppid to confirm they were DataLoader workers from a single training process — NOT a resource conflict.
   - **Disk monitoring**: Tracked from 112GB→98GB free, confirmed healthy for ongoing operations.

   - "[worker_done: worker_analysis_plan_005_stratified_v2_20260514T202757]": Review request for stratified v2 analysis

   - "[worker_done: worker_check_m1_reextract_v2_progress_20260514T210517]": Review request for M1 reextract progress
   - "[worker_done: worker_monitor_progress_check_may15_20260514T211439]": Review request for comprehensive progress check
   - "[worker_done: worker_research_related-work-expansion_20260514T211428]": Review request for related work expansion

   - **Monitor Qwen s44 eval@1000**: Was at step 850, eval@1000 imminent — need to check if R@1=0.6009 holds or regresses

title: \\\\\\\"Effective Hard Negative Mining for Contrastive Learning-Based Code Search\\\\\\\"\\\\n        authors: \\\\\\\"Fan Y., Li C., Ge J., Huang L., Luo B.\\\\\\\"\\\\n        venue: \\\\\\\"ACM TOSEM\\\\\\\"\\\\n        year: 2025\\\\n        relation: contrast-target\\\\n        summary: \\\\\\\"CoCoHaNeRe: 提出可微分 hard negative 机制用于代码搜索对比学习。其对 hard negative 机制的处理与 StructFuse 的发现形成关键对照——StructFuse 证明 true embedding-distance mining 即可持续提升性能。\\\\\\\"\\\\n        note: \\\\\\\"关键引用：StructFuse Claim 1 的 correction target。CoCoHaNeRe 论点是非可微 embedding negatives 学习'slow and ineffective'，StructFuse 证明用 true embedding distance mining 仍然有效。\\\\\\\"\\\\n\\\\n  # ==========================================================================\\\\n  # Category 3: Contrastive Learning for Code\\\\n  # ==========================================================================\\\\n  contrastive_code_learning:\\\\n    description: \\\\\\\"代码领域的对比学习方法，训练时利用代码特性构建正负样本\\\\\\\"\\\\n    papers:\\\\n\\\\n      - {"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"path\": \"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_5/projects/structfuse-context/paper_framing.md\", \"content\": \"# Paper Framing — StructFuse\\n\\n## Target Venue\\n- Primary: EMNLP 2026 Main\\n- Deadline: ARR May 25, 2026\\n\\n## Core Claims (3)\\n\\n1. **Simple static embedding-distance mining suffices for contrastive code retrieval, challenging the assumption that expensive gradient-based hard negative methods are necessary.** CoCoHaNeRe (ACM TOSEM 2025) argues non-differentiable embedding negatives are \\\"slow and ineffective\\\" compared to gradient-based methods — our results show that in structural code retrieval, simple embedding-distance mining yields +1.6–2.0pp R@1 over random baselines across two decoder-only encoders with distinct pretraining corpora and representation geometries (Qwen2.5-Coder-3B, StarCoder2-3B), without requiring gradient-based mechanisms.\\n\\n2. **[Exploratory observation] Structure-grounded (dataflow) negatives interact with encoder representation geometry, but do not consistently outperform embedding mining.** Pilot data shows dataflow < embedding on both encoders (Qwen: -1.2pp, StarCoder: -1.0pp R@1 vs embedding). The interaction pattern — Qwen (saturated, pos_sim > 0.98) shows smaller gap than StarCoder (healthy, pos_sim ~ 0.80) — suggests representation geometry mediates strategy effectiveness. neg_difficulty margin analysis (+17% Qwen, +28% StarCoder) provides mechanistic evidence for this interaction. Pending: multi-seed confirmation + plan_005 stratified analysis + plan_015 dynamic mining control. This is reported as an observation with mechanistic hypothesis, not an established finding.\\n\\n3. **Scope-bounded inter-procedural data-flow analysis via CodeQL provides a practical pipeline for computing structural connectivity at scale.** 0% timeout, 78% connected-not-gold rate (Z%), 123/131 repos with output at full scale. [Dream [M][W] flag: this is supporting methodology, not a primary research contribution. Drop speculative generalization claims (molecular networks, etc.) — no evidence. Frame as practical tool that enables the study, not a standalone contribution.]\\n\\n## Story Spine\\n\\n- **Problem**: Repo-level code repair requires selecting relevant files from large codebases. Current retrievers rely on text similarity alone, but lexical similarity ≠ structural relevance. Hard negative mining is the standard approach to improve contrastive retrievers, yet the field lacks systematic understanding of what negative selection strategies work for code and why.\\n\\n- **Existing approaches & gaps**:\\n  - Text-based retrieval (SweRank, SWE-Fixer): blind to structural dependencies\\n  - Graph-guided at inference (LocAgent, RANGER, CodexGraph): structural graphs at inference only, no training signal\\n  - ARISE: data-flow but intra-procedural only (no call boundary crossing), runtime tool calls only\\n  - CoCoHaNeRe (ACM TOSEM 2025): argues gradient-based hard negative methods outperform non-differentiable embedding negatives — but does not test simple static embedding-distance mining in structural code retrieval settings\\n  - Gap: no systematic study comparing negative mining strategies with proper controls, and no mechanistic explanation for when/why each strategy works\\n\\n- **Our contribution**: A systematic analysis of hard negative mining strategies for contrastive code retrieval. We (1) show that simple static embedding-distance mining suffices, challenging the assumption that expensive gradient-based methods are necessary (CoCoHaNeRe's premise), (2) observe that structure-grounded (dataflow) negatives interact with encoder representation geometry and provide mechanistic analysis of when/why different strategies yield different outcomes, and (3) validate findings on downstream SWE-bench task evaluation.\\n\\n- **Headline result template**: \\\"Simple static embedding-distance mining consistently improves code retrieval (+1.6–2.0pp R@1, 2 encoders), challenging the need for expensive gradient-based hard negative methods. Structure-grounded (dataflow) negatives show encoder-dependent interaction patterns — representation geometry analysis via negative difficulty provides mechanistic explanation for when different mining strategies are effective.\\\"\\n\\n## Core Insight\\n\\nThe effectiveness of hard negative mining strategies depends on the encoder's representation geometry. When the pretrained space is saturated (high pos_sim → low margin between positive and negative), the encoder needs harder negatives from an orthogonal signal dimension (structure). When the space has healthy separation, embedding-distance mining alone captures sufficient difficulty gradient. This encoder-strategy interaction explains contradictory findings in prior work.\\n\\n## Dual-Pillar Motivation\\n\\n1. **Primary — Systematic analysis challenging gradient-based assumptions**: Prior work (CoCoHaNeRe, ACM TOSEM 2025) assumes expensive gradient-based hard negative methods are necessary for effective code search. Our controlled comparison shows simple static embedding-distance mining suffices in structural code retrieval, isolating the effect of each strategy.\\n2. **Secondary — Mechanistic explanation via representation geometry**: neg_difficulty analysis (margin distribution × strategy × encoder) provides explanatory depth beyond empirical comparison, enabling practitioners to predict which strategy suits their encoder without exhaustive experiments.\\n\\n## Formal Definition\\n\\nGiven retrieval domain D, structural graph G, similarity function s, query q:\\n**structure-grounded hard negative** = argmax_{x ∈ D : s(x,q) > τ ∧ ¬reachable_G(x,q)} s(x,q)\\n\\nThe structural graph is externally fixed and does not update with the encoder (drift-immune).\\n\\n## Title Options\\n\\n- Primary: \\\"What Makes Hard Negatives Work for Code Retrieval? A Systematic Study of Mining Strategies and Representation Geometry\\\"\\n- Alternative: \\\"StructFuse: When and Why Structure-Grounded Hard Negatives Improve Contrastive Code Retrieval\\\"\\n- Backup: \\\"Simple Embedding Mining Suffices: Challenging Gradient-Based Assumptions in Hard Negative Mining for Code\\\"\\n\\n## Venue Fit\\n\\n1. **EMNLP Main fit**: Empirical analysis + mechanistic insight papers are a strong EMNLP archetype (cf. \\\"Do Language Models...\\\" papers). Contribution is understanding, not just a new method.\\n2. **NLP audience**: Frame as contrastive learning methodology with code as instantiation. Representation geometry analysis generalizes beyond code.\\n3. **Against \\\"incremental\\\" criticism**: Paper challenges a prevalent assumption + provides mechanistic explanation for strategy × encoder interaction + validates on downstream task. Three contributions, not one marginal improvement.\\n\\n## Anticipated Reviewer Concerns\\n\\n| Concern | Response | Evidence |\\n|---------|----------|----------|\\n| Only 2 encoders — can you generalize? | n=2 is minimum for interaction claim; we frame as observation with mechanism, not universal law; two decoder-only encoders with distinct pretraining (Fill vs FIM) and representation geometry (pos_sim 0.99 vs 0.80) provide meaningful variation | plan_013 (done), m2_full runs |\\n| BM25 vs true embedding negatives? | Run BM25 negatives to empirically compare with embedding-distance mining, grounding the comparison against CoCoHaNeRe's gradient-based premise | plan_018 |\\n| Pool size sensitivity? | Evaluate at pool {50, 100, 200, 500} to confirm strategy rankings hold at realistic sizes | plan_019 |\\n| Stratified by structural data availability? | Report per-stratum R@1 for 34% with structural data vs 66% fallback | plan_005 (extended) |\\n| Temperature regime specificity | Ablate τ ∈ {0.05, 0.1, 0.2} | plan_014 |\\n| No downstream task evaluation | SWE-bench Lite end-to-end with LLM agent | plan_003 |\\n| Static vs dynamic mining confound | Clarify mining is static; add dynamic baseline | plan_015 |\\n| CodeQL scalability at larger scale | 0% timeout, 78% Z%, 123 repos; m1_reextract_v2 adds more | plan_006 (done) |\\n| Why not compare inference-time graph methods? | Complementary: they navigate at inference, we mine at training. Discuss relationship. | plan_017 |\\n| Single benchmark | Add RepoBench-R cross-file retrieval | plan_004 |\\n| Statistical significance | Bootstrap 95% CI for all comparisons; full M2 = 9 seeds × 3 strategies × 2 encoders | plan_016, m2_full |\\n| \\\"Complementary signal\\\" overstatement | Temper: \\\"conditionally beneficial\\\" not \\\"always complementary\\\" | Writing discipline |\\n| differential explains strategy interaction\\n- m2_embed_verify (pilot): 3-strategy comparison confirms ranking (embedding > random, dataflow conditional)\\n- plan_013 (StarCoder pilot): Confirms ```text
1	# 5. Discussion
2	
3	## 5.1 Relationship to Inference-Time Graph Methods
4	
5	A growing body of work leverages code structure graphs at inference time to improve LLM-based software engineering.
6	CodexGraph (Liu et al., NAACL 2025) materializes a code property graph in a Neo4j database that LLM agents query via Cypher; RepoGraph (Ouyang et al., ICLR 2025) provides line-level code graph navigation as a plug-in for SE agents; LocAgent (Chen et al., ACL 2025) and RANGER (Shah et al., 2025) employ graph-guided multi-hop reasoning and MCTS-based traversal for fault localization and retrieval, respectively. GraphCoder (Liu et al., ASE 2024) and ARISE (Seddik & Fard, 2026) come closest to our work in incorporating data-flow information, but both are limited to intra-procedural scope.
7	
8	Table 5 summarizes the key dimensions of comparison. We highlight two axes along which StructFuse occupies a distinct position:
9	
10	**When structural information enters the pipeline.** Inference-time methods construct and query graphs per request, incurring latency proportional to graph complexity and—for agent-based systems—requiring multiple LLM calls per query. StructFuse amortizes all graph computation to training time: CodeQL extraction costs approximately 8.9 seconds per instance (~110 CPU-hours for 44K instances; Table 4), after which the trained encoder operates at standard embedding-lookup speed with zero graph overhead. For latency-sensitive, high-throughput retrieval scenarios, this cost model is substantially more favorable.
11	
12	**Depth of data-flow analysis.** Most inference-time graph methods use structural relations (call graphs, class hierarchies, import dependencies). GraphCoder incorporates control-flow and data-dependence graphs, and ARISE exposes statement-level def-use edges, but both operate within single procedures. StructFuse leverages CodeQL's bounded inter-procedural taint tracking (2-hop call-graph scope), capturing cross-function data-flow paths—e.g., a sanitizer that transforms data between a source and sink across call boundaries. This deeper semantic signal generates harder, more informative negatives than structural proximity alone.
13	
14	**Complementarity.** We emphasize that StructFuse and inference-time graph methods address different stages of the retrieval pipeline and are composable rather than competing. A StructFuse-trained encoder can serve as a first-stage retriever whose embeddings already encode data-flow semantics, reducing the search space for inference-time graph agents that perform targeted reranking. Conversely, systems like CodexGraph or LocAgent can refine StructFuse retrieval results through explicit graph navigation when maximum precision is required. The two paradigms are complementary: StructFuse internalizes general structural patterns during training, while inference-time methods provide case-specific structural reasoning during deployment.
15	
16	## 5.2 Representation Geometry as a Predictor of Strategy Effectiveness
17	
18	Our negative difficulty analysis (Section 4.4) reveals a practical insight: the pretrained encoder's representation geometry predicts whether structure-grounded negatives will provide benefit beyond embedding-distance mining.
19	
20	When the pretrained representation space is saturated—as observed for Qwen2.5-Coder-3B, where mean positive-pair cosine similarity reaches 0.986—the margin between positive and negative pairs is compressed (random margin = 0.074; embedding margin = 0.017). In this regime, all text-similarity-based negatives are clustered near the positive, and the encoder requires negatives drawn from an orthogonal signal dimension (structural disconnection) to learn meaningful discrimination. This explains the +0.8pp R@1 improvement from dataflow-aware negatives over random baselines for Qwen (Table 2).
21	
22	When the representation space has healthy separation—as observed for StarCoder2-3B (pos\_sim = 0.799, random margin = 0.194, 2.6× larger than Qwen)—embedding-distance mining alone provides a sufficient difficulty gradient, and dataflow negatives do not yield additional improvement (+1.6pp for embedding vs. random, but −0.5pp for dataflow vs. random; Table 2). The larger margins mean the encoder can still extract useful contrastive signal from text-similarity-based negatives without supplementary structural guidance.
23	
24	This interaction between representation geometry and strategy effectiveness has a practical implication: practitioners can compute pos\_sim on a held-out sample with their pretrained encoder (a single forward pass, requiring no training) to predict whether structure-grounded negatives are worth the extraction cost. We observe that pos\_sim > 0.98 serves as an approximate threshold above which structural negatives provide conditional benefit, though we note this threshold is derived from two encoder observations and should be validated more broadly.
25	
26	## 5.3 Limitations
27	
28	We identify several limitations of the current study that contextualize our findings:
29	
30	**Encoder scope.** Our experiments use two decoder-only 3B-parameter encoders (Qwen2.5-Coder-3B and StarCoder2-3B). While these models differ meaningfully in pretraining corpus and representation geometry—enabling our interaction analysis—generalizing to encoder-only architectures (e.g., CodeBERT), larger scales, or non-code domains requires further investigation.
31	
32	**Single task.** We evaluate on code retrieval with contrastive training. Whether structure-grounded negatives similarly benefit other code understanding tasks (fault localization, code summarization, clone detection) remains an open question, though the underlying principle—that inter-procedural data-flow provides an orthogonal signal to text similarity—is task-agnostic.
33	
34	**Language and tooling dependency.** Our pipeline relies on CodeQL for inter-procedural data-flow extraction and currently supports Python repositories only. Extending to other languages requires language-specific CodeQL queries, and the dependency on a commercial static analysis tool may limit reproducibility. We note that the methodology generalizes to any tool capable of inter-procedural data-flow analysis (e.g., Joern, Soot).
35	
36	**Static mining.** Our negative mining is performed once before training and remains fixed throughout. Dynamic re-mining—where negatives are refreshed each epoch using the current encoder's embeddings—could yield progressively harder negatives as the model improves. We do not evaluate this variant in the current work and acknowledge the static-vs.-dynamic mining confound as a limitation. We plan to address this in future revisions.
37	
38	**Structural data coverage.** CodeQL successfully extracts data-flow information for 36% of instances across 123 repositories (93.9% repo coverage; Table 4). The remaining instances fall back to embedding-distance negatives. While our results demonstrate that even partial structural coverage yields measurable improvements, higher extraction success rates would likely amplify the benefit.
39	
40	## 5.4 Future Work
41	
42	We identify three directions that follow naturally from our findings, while noting that each requires empirical validation before strong claims can be made:
43	
44	**Hybrid retrieval pipelines.** Integrating a StructFuse-trained encoder as the first-stage retriever within inference-time graph systems (e.g., CodexGraph, RANGER) could combine the efficiency of embedding-based retrieval with the precision of runtime graph navigation. Evaluating such pipelines on end-to-end benchmarks like SWE-bench would quantify the practical value of this complementarity.
45	
46	**Representation-guided strategy selection.** Our observation that pos\_sim predicts strategy effectiveness suggests an automated protocol: given a new encoder, compute representation geometry statistics on a calibration set, then select the appropriate negative mining strategy without exhaustive ablation. Validating this protocol across a wider range of encoders and tasks is a natural next step.
47	
48	**Extension to dynamic and multi-lingual settings.** Adapting the pipeline to dynamically re-mine negatives during training, and extending CodeQL queries to languages beyond Python, would address two of our current limitations. For dynamic mining, the key design question is the re-mining frequency—epoch-level re-mining requires repeated CodeQL extraction, and the cost-benefit tradeoff depends on how quickly the encoder's embedding space evolves during training.
49

Discussion\n\n## 5.1 Relationship to Inference-Time Graph Methods\n\nA growing body of work leverages code structure graphs at inference time to improve LLM-based software engineering.\nCodexGraph (Liu et al., NAACL 2025) materializes a code property graph in a Neo4j database that LLM agents query via Cypher; RepoGraph (Ouyang et al., ICLR 2025) provides line-level code graph navigation as a plug-in for SE agents; LocAgent (Chen et al., ACL 2025) and RANGER (Shah et al., 2025) employ graph-guided multi-hop reasoning and MCTS-based traversal for fault localization and retrieval, respectively. We highlight two axes along which StructFuse occupies a distinct position:\n\n**When structural information enters the pipeline.** Inference-time methods construct and query graphs per request, incurring latency proportional to graph complexity and—for agent-based systems—requiring multiple LLM calls per query. StructFuse amortizes all graph computation to training time: CodeQL extraction costs approximately 8.9 seconds per instance (~110 CPU-hours for 44K instances; Table 4), after which the trained encoder operates at standard embedding-lookup speed with zero graph overhead. For latency-sensitive, high-throughput retrieval scenarios, this cost model is substantially more favorable.\n\n**Depth of data-flow analysis.** Most inference-time graph methods use structural relations (call graphs, class hierarchies, import dependencies). GraphCoder incorporates control-flow and data-dependence graphs, and ARISE exposes statement-level def-use edges, but both operate within single procedures. StructFuse leverages CodeQL's bounded inter-procedural taint tracking (2-hop call-graph scope), capturing cross-function data-flow paths—e.g., a sanitizer that transforms data between a source and sink across call boundaries. This deeper semantic signal generates harder, more informative negatives than structural proximity alone.\n\n**Complementarity.** We emphasize that StructFuse and inference-time graph methods address different stages of the retrieval pipeline and are composable rather than competing. Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. title: \\\\\\\"Effective Hard Negative Mining for Contrastive Learning-Based Code Search\\\\\\\"\\\\n        authors: \\\\\\\"Fan Y., Li C., Ge J., Huang L., Luo B.\\\\\\\"\\\\n        venue: \\\\\\\"ACM TOSEM\\\\\\\"\\\\n        year: 2025\\\\n        relation: contrast-target\\\\n        summary: \\\\\\\"CoCoHaNeRe: 提出可微分 hard negative 机制用于代码搜索对比学习。其对 hard negative 机制的处理与 StructFuse 的发现形成关键对照——StructFuse 证明 true embedding-distance mining 即可持续提升性能。\\\\\\\"\\\\n        note: \\\\\\\"关键引用：StructFuse Claim 1 的 correction target。CoCoHaNeRe 论点是非可微 embedding negatives 学习'slow and ineffective'，StructFuse 证明用 true embedding distance mining 仍然有效。\\\\\\\"\\\\n\\\\n  # ==========================================================================\\\\n  # Category 3: Contrastive Learning for Code\\\\n  # ==========================================================================\\\\n  contrastive_code_learning:\\\\n    description: \\\\\\\"代码领域的对比学习方法，训练时利用代码特性构建正负样本\\\\\\\"\\\\n    papers:\\\\n\\\\n      - title: \\\\\\\"Contrastive Code Representation Learning\\\\\\\"\\\\n        authors: \\\\\\\"Paras Jain, Ajay Jain, Tianjun Zhang, Pieter Abbeel, Joseph Gonzalez, Ion Stoica\\\\\\\"\\\\n        venue: EMNLP\\\\n        year: 2021\\\\n        relation: background\\\\n        summary: \\\\\\\"ContraCode: 自监督对比预训练，通过编译器变换生成功能等价的程序变体，学习代码功能而非表面形式。\\\\\\\"\\\\n\\\\n      - _source line 895 · 2026-05-14T21:34:45.886Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01CHQm6eKRL2J1L319nKHmsa`</summary>

```text
1	# 5. Discussion
2	
3	## 5.1 Relationship to Inference-Time Graph Methods
4	
5	A growing body of work leverages code structure graphs at inference time to improve LLM-based software engineering.
6	CodexGraph (Liu et al., NAACL 2025) materializes a code property graph in a Neo4j database that LLM agents query via Cypher; RepoGraph (Ouyang et al., ICLR 2025) provides line-level code graph navigation as a plug-in for SE agents; LocAgent (Chen et al., ACL 2025) and RANGER (Shah et al., 2025) employ graph-guided multi-hop reasoning and MCTS-based traversal for fault localization and retrieval, respectively. GraphCoder (Liu et al., ASE 2024) and ARISE (Seddik & Fard, 2026) come closest to our work in incorporating data-flow information, but both are limited to intra-procedural scope.
7	
8	Table 5 summarizes the key dimensions of comparison. We highlight two axes along which StructFuse occupies a distinct position:
9	
10	**When structural information enters the pipeline.** Inference-time methods construct and query graphs per request, incurring latency proportional to graph complexity and—for agent-based systems—requiring multiple LLM calls per query. StructFuse amortizes all graph computation to training time: CodeQL extraction costs approximately 8.9 seconds per instance (~110 CPU-hours for 44K instances; Table 4), after which the trained encoder operates at standard embedding-lookup speed with zero graph overhead. For latency-sensitive, high-throughput retrieval scenarios, this cost model is substantially more favorable.
```

</details>

{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"path\": \"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_5/projects/structfuse-context/paper_framing.md\", \"content\": \"# Paper Framing — StructFuse\\n\\n## Target Venue\\n- Primary: EMNLP 2026 Main\\n- Deadline: ARR May 25, 2026\\n\\n## Core Claims (3)\\n\\n1. **Simple static embedding-distance mining suffices for contrastive code retrieval, challenging the assumption that expensive gradient-based hard negative methods are necessary.** CoCoHaNeRe (ACM TOSEM 2025) argues non-differentiable embedding negatives are \\\"slow and ineffective\\\" compared to gradient-based methods — our results show that in structural code retrieval, simple embedding-distance mining yields +1.6–2.0pp R@1 over random baselines across two decoder-only encoders with distinct pretraining corpora and representation geometries (Qwen2.5-Coder-3B, StarCoder2-3B), without requiring gradient-based mechanisms.\\n\\n2. **[Exploratory observation] Structure-grounded (dataflow) negatives interact with encoder representation geometry, but do not consistently outperform embedding mining.** Pilot data shows dataflow < embedding on both encoders (Qwen: -1.2pp, StarCoder: -1.0pp R@1 vs embedding). The interaction pattern — Qwen (saturated, pos_sim > 0.98) shows smaller gap than StarCoder (healthy, pos_sim ~ 0.80) — suggests representation geometry mediates strategy effectiveness. neg_difficulty margin analysis (+17% Qwen, +28% StarCoder) provides mechanistic evidence for this interaction. Pending: multi-seed confirmation + plan_005 stratified analysis + plan_015 dynamic mining control. This is reported as an observation with mechanistic hypothesis, not an established finding.\\n\\n3. **Scope-bounded inter-procedural data-flow analysis via CodeQL provides a practical pipeline for computing structural connectivity at scale.** 0% timeout, 78% connected-not-gold rate (Z%), 123/131 repos with output at full scale. [Dream [M][W] flag: this is supporting methodology, not a primary research contribution. Drop speculative generalization claims (molecular networks, etc.) — no evidence. Frame as practical tool that enables the study, not a standalone contribution.]\\n\\n## Story Spine\\n\\n- **Problem**: Repo-level code repair requires selecting relevant files from large codebases. Current retrievers rely on text similarity alone, but lexical similarity ≠ structural relevance. Hard negative mining is the standard approach to improve contrastive retrievers, yet the field lacks systematic understanding of what negative selection strategies work for code and why.\\n\\n- **Existing approaches & gaps**:\\n  - Text-based retrieval (SweRank, SWE-Fixer): blind to structural dependencies\\n  - Graph-guided at inference (LocAgent, RANGER, CodexGraph): structural graphs at inference only, no training signal\\n  - ARISE: data-flow but intra-procedural only (no call boundary crossing), runtime tool calls only\\n  - CoCoHaNeRe (ACM TOSEM 2025): argues gradient-based hard negative methods outperform non-differentiable embedding negatives — but does not test simple static embedding-distance mining in structural code retrieval settings\\n  - Gap: no systematic study comparing negative mining strategies with proper controls, and no mechanistic explanation for when/why each strategy works\\n\\n- **Our contribution**: A systematic analysis of hard negative mining strategies for contrastive code retrieval. We (1) show that simple static embedding-distance mining suffices, challenging the assumption that expensive gradient-based methods are necessary (CoCoHaNeRe's premise), (2) observe that structure-grounded (dataflow) negatives interact with encoder representation geometry and provide mechanistic analysis of when/why different strategies yield different outcomes, and (3) validate findings on downstream SWE-bench task evaluation.\\n\\n- **Headline result template**: \\\"Simple static embedding-distance mining consistently improves code retrieval (+1.6–2.0pp R@1, 2 encoders), challenging the need for expensive gradient-based hard negative methods. Structure-grounded (dataflow) negatives show encoder-dependent interaction patterns — representation geometry analysis via negative difficulty provides mechanistic explanation for when different mining strategies are effective.\\\"\\n\\n## Core Insight\\n\\nThe effectiveness of hard negative mining strategies depends on the encoder's representation geometry. When the pretrained space is saturated (high pos_sim → low margin between positive and negative), the encoder needs harder negatives from an orthogonal signal dimension (structure). When the space has healthy separation, embedding-distance mining alone captures sufficient difficulty gradient. This encoder-strategy interaction explains contradictory findings in prior work.\\n\\n## Dual-Pillar Motivation\\n\\n1. **Primary — Systematic analysis challenging gradient-based assumptions**: Prior work (CoCoHaNeRe, ACM TOSEM 2025) assumes expensive gradient-based hard negative methods are necessary for effective code search. Our controlled comparison shows simple static embedding-distance mining suffices in structural code retrieval, isolating the effect of each strategy.\\n2. **Secondary — Mechanistic explanation via representation geometry**: neg_difficulty analysis (margin distribution × strategy × encoder) provides explanatory depth beyond empirical comparison, enabling practitioners to predict which strategy suits their encoder without exhaustive experiments.\\n\\n## Formal Definition\\n\\nGiven retrieval domain D, structural graph G, similarity function s, query q:\\n**structure-grounded hard negative** = argmax_{x ∈ D : s(x,q) > τ ∧ ¬reachable_G(x,q)} s(x,q)\\n\\nThe structural graph is externally fixed and does not update with the encoder (drift-immune).\\n\\n## Title Options\\n\\n- Primary: \\\"What Makes Hard Negatives Work for Code Retrieval? A Systematic Study of Mining Strategies and Representation Geometry\\\"\\n- Alternative: \\\"StructFuse: When and Why Structure-Grounded Hard Negatives Improve Contrastive Code Retrieval\\\"\\n- Backup: \\\"Simple Embedding Mining Suffices: Challenging Gradient-Based Assumptions in Hard Negative Mining for Code\\\"\\n\\n## Venue Fit\\n\\n1. **EMNLP Main fit**: Empirical analysis + mechanistic insight papers are a strong EMNLP archetype (cf. \\\"Do Language Models...\\\" papers). Contribution is understanding, not just a new method.\\n2. **NLP audience**: Frame as contrastive learning methodology with code as instantiation. Representation geometry analysis generalizes beyond code.\\n3. **Against \\\"incremental\\\" criticism**: Paper challenges a prevalent assumption + provides mechanistic explanation for strategy × encoder interaction + validates on downstream task. Three contributions, not one marginal improvement.\\n\\n## Anticipated Reviewer Concerns\\n\\n| Concern | Response | Evidence |\\n|---------|----------|----------|\\n| Only 2 encoders — can you generalize? | n=2 is minimum for interaction claim; we frame as observation with mechanism, not universal law; two decoder-only encoders with distinct pretraining (Fill vs FIM) and representation geometry (pos_sim 0.99 vs 0.80) provide meaningful variation | plan_013 (done), m2_full runs |\\n| BM25 vs true embedding negatives? | Run BM25 negatives to empirically compare with embedding-distance mining, grounding the comparison against CoCoHaNeRe's gradient-based premise | plan_018 |\\n| Pool size sensitivity? | Evaluate at pool {50, 100, 200, 500} to confirm strategy rankings hold at realistic sizes | plan_019 |\\n| Stratified by structural data availability? | Report per-stratum R@1 for 34% with structural data vs 66% fallback | plan_005 (extended) |\\n| Temperature regime specificity | Ablate τ ∈ {0.05, 0.1, 0.2} | plan_014 |\\n| No downstream task evaluation | SWE-bench Lite end-to-end with LLM agent | plan_003 |\\n| Static vs dynamic mining confound | Clarify mining is static; add dynamic baseline | plan_015 |\\n| CodeQL scalability at larger scale | 0% timeout, 78% Z%, 123 repos; m1_reextract_v2 adds more | plan_006 (done) |\\n| Why not compare inference-time graph methods? | Complementary: they navigate at inference, we mine at training. Discuss relationship. | plan_017 |\\n| Single benchmark | Add RepoBench-R cross-file retrieval | plan_004 |\\n| Statistical significance | Bootstrap 95% CI for all comparisons; full M2 = 9 seeds × 3 strategies × 2 encoders | plan_016, m2_full |\\n| \\\"Complementary signal\\\" overstatement | Temper: \\\"conditionally beneficial\\\" not \\\"always complementary\\\" | Writing discipline |\\n| Missing related work | RepoFusion, GRACE, GraphCodeBERT, ANCE, curriculum HN mining | Writing task |\\n\\n## Evidence Status\\n\\n### Confirmed findings (basis for claims):\\n- D017: True embedding mining helps (+2.0pp Qwen, +1.6pp StarCoder) — challenges gradient-based necessity assumption\\n- D021/neg_difficulty: Qwen pos_sim=0.9855 (saturated), StarCoder pos_sim=0.7985 (healthy); margin differential explains strategy interaction\\n- m2_embed_verify (pilot): 3-strategy comparison confirms ranking (embedding > random, dataflow conditional)\\n- plan_013 (StarCoder pilot): Confirms cross-encoder pattern — embedding helps both, dataflow helps Qwen only\\n- plan_006: CodeQL scalability confirmed at 123-repo scale\\n\\n### In progress:\\n- m2_full_qwen: 9 seeds × 3 strategies (seed We (1) show that simple static embedding-distance mining suffices, challenging the assumption that expensive gradient-based methods are necessary (CoCoHaNeRe's premise), (2) observe that structure-grounded (dataflow) negatives interact with encoder representation geometry and provide mechanistic analysis of when/why different strategies yield different outcomes, and (3) validate findings on downstream SWE-bench task evaluation.\\\\n\\\\n- **Headline result template**: \\\\\\\"Simple static embedding-distance mining consistently improves code retrieval (+1.6–2.0pp R@1, 2 encoders), challenging the need for expensive gradient-based hard negative methods. Structure-grounded (dataflow) negatives show encoder-dependent interaction patterns — representation geometry analysis via negative difficulty provides mechanistic explanation for when different mining strategies are effective.\\\\\\\"\\\\n\\\\n## Core Insight\\\\n\\\\nThe effectiveness of hard negative mining strategies depends on the encoder's representation geometry. When the pretrained space is saturated (high pos_sim → low margin between positive and negative), the encoder needs harder negatives from an orthogonal signal dimension (structure). When the space has healthy separation, embedding-distance mining alone captures sufficient difficulty gradient. This encoder-strategy interaction explains contradictory findings in prior work.\\\\n\\\\n## Dual-Pillar Motivation\\\\n\\\\n1. **Primary — Systematic analysis challenging gradient-based assumptions**: Prior work (CoCoHaNeRe, ACM TOSEM 2025) assumes expensive gradient-based hard negative methods are necessary for effective code search. Our controlled comparison shows simple static embedding-distance mining suffices in structural code retrieval, isolating the effect of each strategy.\\\\n2. **Secondary — Mechanistic explanation via representation geometry**: neg_difficulty analysis (margin distribution × strategy × encoder) provides explanatory depth beyond empirical comparison, enabling practitioners to predict which strategy suits their encoder without exhaustive experiments.\\\\n\\\\n## Formal Definition\\\\n\\\\nGiven retrieval domain D, structural graph G, similarity function s, query q:\\\\n**structure-grounded hard negative** = argmax_{x ∈ D : s(x,q) > τ ∧ ¬reachable_G(x,q)} s(x,q)\\\\n\\\\nThe structural graph is externally fixed and does not update with the encoder (drift-immune).\\\\n\\\\n## Title Options\\\\n\\\\n- Primary: \\\\\\\"What Makes Hard Negatives Work for Code Retrieval? A Systematic Study of Mining Strategies and Representation Geometry\\\\\\\"\\\\n- Alternative: \\\\\\\"StructFuse: When and Why Structure-Grounded Hard Negatives Improve Contrastive Code Retrieval\\\\\\\"\\\\n- Backup: \\\\\\\"Simple Embedding Mining Suffices: Challenging Gradient-Based Assumptions in Hard Negative Mining for Code\\\\\\\"\\\\n\\\\n## Venue Fit\\\\n\\\\n1. **EMNLP Main fit**: Empirical analysis + mechanistic insight papers are a strong EMNLP archetype (cf. \\\\\\\"Do Language Models...\\\\\\\" papers). D017\\\\n- id: D019\\\\n  date: 2026-05-13 18:08 UTC\\\\n  summary: D018 补充：Full M2 go/no-go 量化阈值 + 统计检验方案\\\\n  rationale: 'Reviewer 指出 D018 缺量化阈值，go/no-go 判据需在结果出来前明确，避免事后 rationalization。\\\\n\\\\n\\\\n    **统计方法**：每个 encoder 3 seeds 的 R@1 (best-by-val-loss checkpoint) 做 paired difference，bootstrap\\\\n    95% CI + paired t-test (p<0.05)。同时报告 best-by-R@1 checkpoint 结果作为透明度补充。\\\\n\\\\n\\\\n    **Go/No-Go 阈值**：\\\\n\\\\n    (A) Go — 原 framing 可用：dataflow > random 的 95% CI 在两个 encoder 上均 > 0\\\\n\\\\n    (B) Conditional Go — 修正 framing：dataflow > random 在至少一个 encoder 上 CI > 0，另一个 CI\\\\n    含 0（差异不显著）\\\\n\\\\n    (C) No-Go for Claim 1：dataflow ≤ random 在两个 encoder 上 CI 均含 0 或为负 → Claim 1 失效，pivot\\\\n    到 \\\\\\\"mining strategy × architecture\\\\\\\" 分析论文\\\\n\\\\n\\\\n    **Claim 2 判据**：若 full M2 显示 embedding > random 且 CI > 0 → 原 Claim 2 (\\\\\\\"embedding\\\\n    harms\\\\\\\") 被否定。新 Claim 2 候选：\\\\\\\"true embedding mining helps, but BM25 proxy harms\\\\\\\" 或\\\\n    \\\\\\\"hard negative mining method matters more than hard negative source\\\\\\\"\\\\n\\\\n\\\\n    **跨 encoder 一致性规则**：\\\\n\\\\n    - 同方向同显著 → 强泛化 claim\\\\n\\\\n    - 同方向不同显著 → 条件 claim + architecture discussion\\\\n\\\\n    - 反方向 → Claim 1 原形式被拒，需 conditional reframe\\\\n\\\\n\\\\n    阈值在 full M2 结果出来前锁定，不允许事后修改。'\\\\n  related_experiments:\\\\n  - D017\\n- id: D019\\n  date: 2026-05-13 18:08 UTC\\n  summary: D018 补充：Full M2 go/no-go 量化阈值 + 统计检验方案\\n  rationale: 'Reviewer 指出 D018 缺量化阈值，go/no-go 判据需在结果出来前明确，避免事后 rationalization。\\n\\n\\n    **统计方法**：每个 encoder 3 seeds 的 R@1 (best-by-val-loss checkpoint) 做 paired difference，bootstrap\\n    95% CI + paired t-test (p<0.05)。同时报告 best-by-R@1 checkpoint 结果作为透明度补充。\\n\\n\\n    **Go/No-Go 阈值**：\\n\\n    (A) Go — 原 framing 可用：dataflow > random 的 95% CI 在两个 encoder 上均 > 0\\n\\n    (B) Conditional Go — 修正 framing：dataflow > random 在至少一个 encoder 上 CI > 0，另一个 CI\\n    含 0（差异不显著）\\n\\n    (C) No-Go for Claim 1：dataflow ≤ random 在两个 encoder 上 CI 均含 0 或为负 → Claim 1 失效，pivot\\n    到 \\\"mining strategy × architecture\\\" 分析论文\\n\\n\\n    **Claim 2 判据**：若 full M2 显示 embedding > random 且 CI > 0 → 原 Claim 2 (\\\"embedding\\n    harms\\\") 被否定。新 Claim 2 候选：\\\"true embedding mining helps, but BM25 proxy harms\\\" 或\\n    \\\"hard negative mining method matters more than hard negative source\\\"\\n\\n\\n    **跨 encoder 一致性规则**：\\n\\n    - 同方向同显著 → 强泛化 claim\\n\\n    - 同方向不同显著 → 条件 claim + architecture discussion\\n\\n    - 反方向 → Claim 1 原形式被拒，需 conditional reframe\\n\\n\\n    阈值在 full M2 结果出来前锁定，不允许事后修改。'\\n  related_experiments:\\n  - df=2 时 t 临界值 4.303，pilot 级别差异（<2pp）极难达到 p<0.05\\n\\n    - Bootstrap n=3 仅 27 种重采样，CI 估计粗糙\\n\\n    - 阈值框架偏向 No-Go（Type II error 高）\\n\\n    - 若结果落入 (C) No-Go，论文 Discussion 必须讨论功效限制，不能简单声明 \\\"dataflow 无效\\\"\\n\\n    - ### Core Claims
1. Claim 1: "Simple static embedding-distance mining suffices for contrastive code retrieval, challenging the assumption that expensive gradient-based hard negative methods are necessary." ✅ New framing.


2. The references check out—CoCoHaNeRe's critique of non-differentiable embedding negatives as slow and ineffective is properly cited, and the empirical gains of 1.6–2.0pp R@1 without gradient-based mechanisms support this reframing. ✅

### Story Spine
- **Problem**: "Hard negative mining is the standard approach to..."

The problem statement now emphasizes the systematic gap—the field lacks understanding of what negative selection strategies actually work for code and why. ✅

- **Existing approaches & gaps**: CoCoHaNeRe argues gradient-based methods outperform non-differentiable embedding negatives, but doesn't test simple static embedding-distance mining in structural code retrieval. The gap is clear: no systematic comparison with proper controls, and no mechanistic explanation for when and why each strategy succeeds. ✅

- **Our contribution**: We show that simple static embedding-distance mining is sufficient, directly challenging the assumption that expensive gradient-based methods are necessary. ✅

- **Headline result**: "challenging the need for expensive gradient-based hard negative methods" ✅

### Core Insight
Now examining what makes hard negative mining strategies effective...

the answer lies in the encoder's representation geometry. This mechanism explains why different strategies work differently—it's the deeper insight that supports both the empirical finding (embedding mining suffices) and the exploratory observation about when dataflow negatives work conditionally. The interaction between strategy and encoder geometry is what really matters here.

### Dual-Pillar Motivation

I'm settling on two complementary angles: the primary one is a systematic analysis that challenges gradient-based assumptions, and the secondary one is the mechanistic explanation through representation geometry.

### Title Options

The primary title should be neutral and systematic: "What Makes Hard Negatives Work for Code Retrieval? A Systematic Study of Mining Strategies and Representation Geometry." The alternative framing around StructFuse doesn't quite work because it implies dataflow consistently improves performance, but that's exploratory and not established. The backup option—"Simple Embedding Mining Suffices: Challenging Gradient-Based Assumptions in Hard Negative Mining for Code"—actually aligns better with the new framing.

### Venue Fit

The paper challenges a common assumption, explains the mechanism behind strategy and encoder interactions, and validates the findings on downstream tasks.

### Anticipated Reviewer Concerns

I'm working through potential objections: the encoder generalization question ties back to the interaction claim, the BM25 comparison grounds itself against the gradient-based premise, and the remaining concerns around pool size sensitivity, structural data availability, temperature regimes, downstream evaluation, static versus dynamic mining confounds, CodeQL scalability, and inference comparisons are all neutral territory.

### Evidence Status

The true embedding mining results show meaningful gains across models, directly challenging the assumption that gradient-based approaches are necessary.

### Contingency

If all 18 runs confirm the pilot pattern, that's strong validation. If the dataflow benefit disappears at scale even for Qwen, I pivot to a two-claim paper focused on what actually holds up.

The core narrative becomes: embedding mining alone is sufficient to challenge the gradient-based assumption, CodeQL extraction proves practical, and the representation geometry analysis explains why prior work reached different conclusions.

If BM25 negatives perform like random sampling, I need to adjust how I frame the gradient-based assumption challenge. And if embedding mining also loses its edge at full scale, that's another constraint on the claims.

The logical flow works — Claim 1 establishes the empirical finding, Claim 2 explains the mechanism behind why certain strategies succeed or fail, and Claim 3 grounds it in infrastructure. The Core Insight already bridges this nicely by noting that when encoder separation is healthy, embedding mining alone captures sufficient difficulty gradient, which ties back to why Claim 1 holds.

Now I'm looking at the D030 contingency framing around BM25 negatives being approximately random rather than harmful, which needs narrative adjustment.

The key insight here is that BM25 being non-gradient and sparse doesn't directly undermine the claim about gradient-based methods being unnecessary — instead, it reinforces it. Even when simple sparse matching like BM25 provides minimal signal, embedding-distance mining still outperforms, which strengthens the argument that gradient-based approaches aren't required.

I'm also checking the Claim 2 data showing that dataflow underperforms embedding mining across both encoders, with specific performance drops noted.

However, the alternative title creates a misalignment — it suggests the dataflow improvement is already established, which contradicts Claim 2's exploratory framing. The anticipated reviewer concerns section correctly addresses the BM25 question.

I notice the evidence status tracking is slightly outdated (mentions seed 1 running when seeds 42 and 43 are already complete), and there's a subtle tension between the formal definition of "structure-grounded hard negative" and the headline claim about embedding-distance mining — the definition actually supports Claim 2 rather than Claim 1, which isn't necessarily problematic since the paper still uses structure-grounded negatives experimentally.
```

practical at scale → enables the study\n\n层次关系在所有相关段落（Core Claims、Our contribution、Dual-Pillar Motivation、Venue Fit）中一致。Core Insight 段聚焦 Claim 2 的机制解释，但末句 \"embedding-distance mining alone captures
